{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01VnYsg9w5RcuRxYVN79arXP","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→# 001 – Architectural Style: Microservices\n     2→\n     3→Status: **Accepted**  \n     4→Date: 2023-08-29  \n     5→ADR-Owner: Architecture Guild  \n     6→\n     7→---\n     8→\n     9→## 1. Context\n    10→\n    11→OpsForge Utility Nexus aims to provide a *single, versioned façade* that aggregates a wide variety of utilities—file-format conversion, data anonymisation, checksum generation, time-zone-aware scheduling, etc. Each utility has a distinct lifecycle, scalability profile, and set of external dependencies.\n    12→\n    13→Historically, similar platforms have been built as:\n    14→\n    15→* a **monolith**, offering a single deployment artefact, or  \n    16→* a **modular monolith**, separating code at compile-time while sharing the same runtime and database schema.\n    17→\n    18→Early spike implementations revealed several pain-points:\n    19→\n    20→* **Resource Contention** – CPU-intensive converters starved IO-bound schedulers.  \n    21→* **Release Cadence Mismatch** – checksum algorithms evolve slowly; anonymisation libraries update weekly.  \n    22→* **Polyglot Dependencies** – some utilities require native binaries; others depend on JDBC drivers.  \n    23→* **Regulatory Isolation** – GDPR mandates data-anonymisation modules to undergo specific audits, blocking the entire delivery pipeline.\n    24→\n    25→Given these concerns—and the fact that customers frequently request *only one or two* utilities—we need an architectural style that supports:\n    26→\n    27→* Independent release and deployment cadences  \n    28→* Autonomous horizontal scaling  \n    29→* Technology heterogeneity per utility  \n    30→* Fine-grained security boundaries  \n    31→* Evolutionary replacement of utilities without cross-cutting regression risk  \n    32→\n    33→## 2. Decision\n    34→\n    35→We will architect OpsForge Utility Nexus as a **Microservice-oriented System**, in which *each utility function is implemented as its own microservice* that:\n    36→\n    37→1. Exposes its capabilities via a lightweight REST and (optionally) GraphQL API.  \n    38→2. Owns its **hexagonal architecture** internals—domain, application services, inbound/outbound adapters.  \n    39→3. Communicates with other services only through well-defined, versioned contracts (HTTP/JSON and/or gRPC where streaming is required).  \n    40→4. Publishes domain events on an event bus for eventual consistency and observability.  \n    41→5. Is deployed, scaled, and monitored independently, yet discovered and composed by the **API Gateway** to present a cohesive façade to consumers.\n    42→\n    43→### Illustrative Directory Layout\n    44→\n    45→```text\n    46→opsforge-utility-nexus/\n    47→├── gateway/               # Spring Cloud Gateway + GraphQL Federation\n    48→├── utilities/\n    49→│   ├── checksum/\n    50→│   ├── converter/\n    51→│   ├── anonymizer/\n    52→│   └── scheduler/\n    53→└── shared-kernel/         # Value objects, error codes, cross-service contracts\n    54→```\n    55→\n    56→### Minimal Service Contract Example (Checksum Utility)\n    57→\n    58→```java\n    59→// inbound adapter\n    60→@RestController\n    61→@RequestMapping(\"/v1/checksum\")\n    62→@RequiredArgsConstructor\n    63→class ChecksumController {\n    64→\n    65→    private final ChecksumUseCase checksumUseCase;\n    66→\n    67→    @PostMapping\n    68→    ResponseEntity<ChecksumResponse> calculate(@Valid @RequestBody ChecksumRequest request) {\n    69→        ChecksumResult result = checksumUseCase.calculate(request.toDomain());\n    70→        return ResponseEntity.ok(ChecksumResponse.from(result));\n    71→    }\n    72→}\n    73→```\n    74→\n    75→## 3. Rationale\n    76→\n    77→* **Decoupled Deployment** – Teams push features without synchronising global release trains.  \n    78→* **Scalability Alignment** – CPU-bound converters can autoscale independently of latency-sensitive schedulers.  \n    79→* **Failure Containment** – Circuit-breaking a malfunctioning anonymisation service leaves other utilities unaffected.  \n    80→* **Technology Freedom** – Teams may select optimal persistence (e.g., PostgreSQL vs MongoDB) or caching layers (e.g., Caffeine vs Redis) without impacting peers.  \n    81→* **Regulatory Compliance** – Security reviews and audits can scope to the service that touches sensitive data.\n    82→\n    83→## 4. Consequences\n    84→\n    85→Pros:\n    86→\n    87→* Faster, risk-isolated releases  \n    88→* Resilience via bulkheads, retries, timeouts  \n    89→* Fine-grained autoscaling, leading to cost optimisation  \n    90→* Facilitates domain-driven design and bounded contexts  \n    91→\n    92→Cons / Trade-offs:\n    93→\n    94→* **Operational Overhead** – More CI pipelines, Docker images, Helm charts, dashboards, alerts.  \n    95→* **Distributed Complexity** – Requires centralised logging, tracing, service discovery, and advanced DevSecOps maturity.  \n    96→* **Data Consistency** – Cross-service transactions demand eventual consistency or Saga patterns.  \n    97→* **Cross-Cutting Concerns Duplication** – Rate-limiting, auth, and caching must be standardised via libraries or sidecars.\n    98→\n    99→Mitigations:\n   100→\n   101→* Provide a **Golden Path** with base images, Terraform modules, Helm templates, and shared Gradle plugins.  \n   102→* Adopt **OpenTelemetry** for unified traces, metrics, and logs.  \n   103→* Leverage **GraphQL Federation** to stitch service schemas and reduce REST round-trips.  \n   104→* Enforce **Semantic Versioning** and **Consumer-Driven Contract Tests** (Pact) to detect breaking changes early.\n   105→\n   106→## 5. Alternatives Considered\n   107→\n   108→| Candidate                      | Why Rejected                                                                 |\n   109→|--------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|\n   110→| Monolith                       | Single blast radius; divergent scalability needs cause over-provisioning     |\n   111→| Modular Monolith               | Better boundaries than classic monolith, but still shares runtime & DB       |\n   112→| Functions-as-a-Service (FaaS)  | Cold-start latency unacceptable for bulk conversions; complex orchestration  |\n   113→| Self-contained Systems (SCS)   | Similar benefits but less tooling support compared to mainstream microservices |\n   114→\n   115→## 6. Related Decisions\n   116→\n   117→* 002 – **Hexagonal Architecture Enforcement**  \n   118→* 003 – **API Gateway & GraphQL Federation Strategy**  \n   119→* 004 – **Event Streaming Backbone (Kafka)**  \n   120→* 005 – **Observability Stack Selection**  \n   121→\n   122→---\n   123→\n   124→> *This ADR is irrevocable only as long as its motivation remains valid. Should empirical evidence prove otherwise, it may be superseded following established governance procedures.*\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"6e40b04a-4c07-4ffd-908b-aa0f7bc54884","uuid":"7de9ec2b-e8e6-48a6-83c7-306def825a1e","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/project/docs/adr/001-architectural-style-microservices.md","content":"# 001 – Architectural Style: Microservices\n\nStatus: **Accepted**  \nDate: 2023-08-29  \nADR-Owner: Architecture Guild  \n\n---\n\n## 1. Context\n\nOpsForge Utility Nexus aims to provide a *single, versioned façade* that aggregates a wide variety of utilities—file-format conversion, data anonymisation, checksum generation, time-zone-aware scheduling, etc. Each utility has a distinct lifecycle, scalability profile, and set of external dependencies.\n\nHistorically, similar platforms have been built as:\n\n* a **monolith**, offering a single deployment artefact, or  \n* a **modular monolith**, separating code at compile-time while sharing the same runtime and database schema.\n\nEarly spike implementations revealed several pain-points:\n\n* **Resource Contention** – CPU-intensive converters starved IO-bound schedulers.  \n* **Release Cadence Mismatch** – checksum algorithms evolve slowly; anonymisation libraries update weekly.  \n* **Polyglot Dependencies** – some utilities require native binaries; others depend on JDBC drivers.  \n* **Regulatory Isolation** – GDPR mandates data-anonymisation modules to undergo specific audits, blocking the entire delivery pipeline.\n\nGiven these concerns—and the fact that customers frequently request *only one or two* utilities—we need an architectural style that supports:\n\n* Independent release and deployment cadences  \n* Autonomous horizontal scaling  \n* Technology heterogeneity per utility  \n* Fine-grained security boundaries  \n* Evolutionary replacement of utilities without cross-cutting regression risk  \n\n## 2. Decision\n\nWe will architect OpsForge Utility Nexus as a **Microservice-oriented System**, in which *each utility function is implemented as its own microservice* that:\n\n1. Exposes its capabilities via a lightweight REST and (optionally) GraphQL API.  \n2. Owns its **hexagonal architecture** internals—domain, application services, inbound/outbound adapters.  \n3. Communicates with other services only through well-defined, versioned contracts (HTTP/JSON and/or gRPC where streaming is required).  \n4. Publishes domain events on an event bus for eventual consistency and observability.  \n5. Is deployed, scaled, and monitored independently, yet discovered and composed by the **API Gateway** to present a cohesive façade to consumers.\n\n### Illustrative Directory Layout\n\n```text\nopsforge-utility-nexus/\n├── gateway/               # Spring Cloud Gateway + GraphQL Federation\n├── utilities/\n│   ├── checksum/\n│   ├── converter/\n│   ├── anonymizer/\n│   └── scheduler/\n└── shared-kernel/         # Value objects, error codes, cross-service contracts\n```\n\n### Minimal Service Contract Example (Checksum Utility)\n\n```java\n// inbound adapter\n@RestController\n@RequestMapping(\"/v1/checksum\")\n@RequiredArgsConstructor\nclass ChecksumController {\n\n    private final ChecksumUseCase checksumUseCase;\n\n    @PostMapping\n    ResponseEntity<ChecksumResponse> calculate(@Valid @RequestBody ChecksumRequest request) {\n        ChecksumResult result = checksumUseCase.calculate(request.toDomain());\n        return ResponseEntity.ok(ChecksumResponse.from(result));\n    }\n}\n```\n\n## 3. Rationale\n\n* **Decoupled Deployment** – Teams push features without synchronising global release trains.  \n* **Scalability Alignment** – CPU-bound converters can autoscale independently of latency-sensitive schedulers.  \n* **Failure Containment** – Circuit-breaking a malfunctioning anonymisation service leaves other utilities unaffected.  \n* **Technology Freedom** – Teams may select optimal persistence (e.g., PostgreSQL vs MongoDB) or caching layers (e.g., Caffeine vs Redis) without impacting peers.  \n* **Regulatory Compliance** – Security reviews and audits can scope to the service that touches sensitive data.\n\n## 4. Consequences\n\nPros:\n\n* Faster, risk-isolated releases  \n* Resilience via bulkheads, retries, timeouts  \n* Fine-grained autoscaling, leading to cost optimisation  \n* Facilitates domain-driven design and bounded contexts  \n\nCons / Trade-offs:\n\n* **Operational Overhead** – More CI pipelines, Docker images, Helm charts, dashboards, alerts.  \n* **Distributed Complexity** – Requires centralised logging, tracing, service discovery, and advanced DevSecOps maturity.  \n* **Data Consistency** – Cross-service transactions demand eventual consistency or Saga patterns.  \n* **Cross-Cutting Concerns Duplication** – Rate-limiting, auth, and caching must be standardised via libraries or sidecars.\n\nMitigations:\n\n* Provide a **Golden Path** with base images, Terraform modules, Helm templates, and shared Gradle plugins.  \n* Adopt **OpenTelemetry** for unified traces, metrics, and logs.  \n* Leverage **GraphQL Federation** to stitch service schemas and reduce REST round-trips.  \n* Enforce **Semantic Versioning** and **Consumer-Driven Contract Tests** (Pact) to detect breaking changes early.\n\n## 5. Alternatives Considered\n\n| Candidate                      | Why Rejected                                                                 |\n|--------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|\n| Monolith                       | Single blast radius; divergent scalability needs cause over-provisioning     |\n| Modular Monolith               | Better boundaries than classic monolith, but still shares runtime & DB       |\n| Functions-as-a-Service (FaaS)  | Cold-start latency unacceptable for bulk conversions; complex orchestration  |\n| Self-contained Systems (SCS)   | Similar benefits but less tooling support compared to mainstream microservices |\n\n## 6. Related Decisions\n\n* 002 – **Hexagonal Architecture Enforcement**  \n* 003 – **API Gateway & GraphQL Federation Strategy**  \n* 004 – **Event Streaming Backbone (Kafka)**  \n* 005 – **Observability Stack Selection**  \n\n---\n\n> *This ADR is irrevocable only as long as its motivation remains valid. Should empirical evidence prove otherwise, it may be superseded following established governance procedures.*","numLines":124,"startLine":1,"totalLines":124}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01BYCiMW9nbVcetHuRtWkgm2","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→```md\n     2→# 002 — Hexagonal Architecture for Services\n     3→> Status: **Accepted**  \n     4→> Date: 2024-06-16  \n     5→> Decision Makers: Platform Architecture Guild  \n     6→> Supersedes: N/A  \n     7→> Tags: architecture, hexagonal, ports-and-adapters\n     8→\n     9→## 1. Context\n    10→OpsForge Utility Nexus exposes a suite of *utility micro-services* ranging from file conversions to bulk text transformations.  \n    11→We need each service to be:\n    12→\n    13→* Technology-agnostic at its core  \n    14→* Swappable in-/out-bound infrastructure without touching business logic  \n    15→* Meticulously testable (pure unit tests at the core, integration tests at the edges)  \n    16→* Compatible with both HTTP/JSON and GraphQL façades  \n    17→\n    18→These goals naturally align with **Hexagonal Architecture (aka Ports & Adapters)**.\n    19→\n    20→## 2. Decision\n    21→Every utility is implemented as a self-contained hexagon:\n    22→\n    23→1. **Domain/Core**  \n    24→   * Pure POJOs and value objects  \n    25→   * Use-case orchestrators (Application Services)  \n    26→   * Port interfaces (inbound & outbound)\n    27→\n    28→2. **Adapters**  \n    29→   * **Inbound**: REST controllers / GraphQL resolvers / message listeners  \n    30→   * **Outbound**: Repository drivers, SaaS connectors, cache providers  \n    31→\n    32→3. **Configuration**  \n    33→   * Wiring of ports to adapters via dependency injection (Spring Boot)  \n    34→   * Uniform, versioned contracts exposed by the API Gateway layer  \n    35→\n    36→All cross-cutting concerns (rate limiting, caching, tracing, etc.) are expressed as *decorators* or *framework interceptors*, never leaking into domain code.\n    37→\n    38→## 3. Consequences\n    39→✔ Fast unit tests against core logic  \n    40→✔ Easy to swap technologies (e.g., Caffeine → Redis)  \n    41→✔ Clear test boundaries (mock ports instead of mocks everywhere)  \n    42→✘ Slightly higher initial boilerplate (worth the ROI)\n    43→\n    44→---\n    45→\n    46→## 4. Reference Implementation\n    47→\n    48→Below is a trimmed, yet **runnable** example of the File-Conversion utility adopting the hexagonal style.\n    49→\n    50→```java\n    51→// -------- 1️⃣ Domain Layer (Core) -------------------------------------------\n    52→package com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain;\n    53→\n    54→import java.time.OffsetDateTime;\n    55→import java.util.UUID;\n    56→\n    57→/**\n    58→ * Value object that represents a single file-conversion request.\n    59→ */\n    60→public record ConversionRequest(\n    61→        UUID requestId,\n    62→        String sourceFormat,\n    63→        String targetFormat,\n    64→        byte[] payload,\n    65→        OffsetDateTime requestedAt\n    66→) {}\n    67→\n    68→/**\n    69→ * Port that expresses the *purpose* of persisting conversion artefacts.\n    70→ */\n    71→public interface ArtefactStoragePort {\n    72→    void save(UUID requestId, byte[] convertedPayload);\n    73→}\n    74→\n    75→/**\n    76→ * Primary use-case orchestrator (Application Service).\n    77→ */\n    78→public class ConvertFileUseCase {\n    79→\n    80→    private final ArtefactStoragePort storagePort;\n    81→    private final FileFormatConverter converter; // pure domain service\n    82→\n    83→    public ConvertFileUseCase(ArtefactStoragePort storagePort,\n    84→                              FileFormatConverter converter) {\n    85→        this.storagePort = storagePort;\n    86→        this.converter   = converter;\n    87→    }\n    88→\n    89→    /**\n    90→     * Executes the conversion and persists the output using the outbound port.\n    91→     */\n    92→    public ConversionResponse execute(ConversionRequest request) {\n    93→        byte[] output = converter.convert(\n    94→                request.sourceFormat(),\n    95→                request.targetFormat(),\n    96→                request.payload()\n    97→        );\n    98→        storagePort.save(request.requestId(), output);\n    99→        return new ConversionResponse(request.requestId(), output.length);\n   100→    }\n   101→}\n   102→\n   103→/**\n   104→ * Domain service that performs format conversion.\n   105→ * Decoupled from any IO concerns.\n   106→ */\n   107→public interface FileFormatConverter {\n   108→    byte[] convert(String from, String to, byte[] input);\n   109→}\n   110→\n   111→/**\n   112→ * Minimal response DTO emitted by the use-case.\n   113→ */\n   114→public record ConversionResponse(UUID requestId, int sizeInBytes) {}\n   115→```\n   116→\n   117→```java\n   118→// -------- 2️⃣ Inbound Adapter (REST Controller) -----------------------------\n   119→package com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.inbound.rest;\n   120→\n   121→import com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain.*;\n   122→import jakarta.validation.constraints.NotNull;\n   123→import org.springframework.http.ResponseEntity;\n   124→import org.springframework.validation.annotation.Validated;\n   125→import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.*;\n   126→\n   127→import java.time.OffsetDateTime;\n   128→import java.util.UUID;\n   129→\n   130→/**\n   131→ * REST façade that delegates to the core use-case.\n   132→ */\n   133→@RestController\n   134→@RequestMapping(\"/v1/conversions\")\n   135→@Validated\n   136→public class FileConversionController {\n   137→\n   138→    private final ConvertFileUseCase useCase;\n   139→\n   140→    public FileConversionController(ConvertFileUseCase useCase) {\n   141→        this.useCase = useCase;\n   142→    }\n   143→\n   144→    @PostMapping\n   145→    public ResponseEntity<ConversionResponse> convert(\n   146→            @RequestParam @NotNull String sourceFormat,\n   147→            @RequestParam @NotNull String targetFormat,\n   148→            @RequestBody byte[] payload\n   149→    ) {\n   150→        ConversionRequest request = new ConversionRequest(\n   151→                UUID.randomUUID(),\n   152→                sourceFormat,\n   153→                targetFormat,\n   154→                payload,\n   155→                OffsetDateTime.now()\n   156→        );\n   157→        return ResponseEntity.ok(useCase.execute(request));\n   158→    }\n   159→}\n   160→```\n   161→\n   162→```java\n   163→// -------- 3️⃣ Outbound Adapter (S3 Artefact Storage) ------------------------\n   164→package com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.outbound.s3;\n   165→\n   166→import com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain.ArtefactStoragePort;\n   167→import org.slf4j.Logger;\n   168→import org.slf4j.LoggerFactory;\n   169→import software.amazon.awssdk.core.sync.RequestBody;\n   170→import software.amazon.awssdk.services.s3.S3Client;\n   171→import software.amazon.awssdk.services.s3.model.PutObjectRequest;\n   172→\n   173→import java.util.UUID;\n   174→\n   175→/**\n   176→ * Concrete adapter that stores conversion outputs in an S3 bucket.\n   177→ */\n   178→public class S3ArtefactStorageAdapter implements ArtefactStoragePort {\n   179→\n   180→    private static final Logger LOG = LoggerFactory.getLogger(S3ArtefactStorageAdapter.class);\n   181→\n   182→    private final S3Client s3;\n   183→    private final String   bucketName;\n   184→\n   185→    public Context\nOpsForge Utility Nexus exposes a suite of *utility micro-services* ranging from file conversions to bulk text transformations.  \nWe need each service to be:\n\n* Technology-agnostic at its core  \n* Swappable in-/out-bound infrastructure without touching business logic  \n* Meticulously testable (pure unit tests at the core, integration tests at the edges)  \n* Compatible with both HTTP/JSON and GraphQL façades  \n\nThese goals naturally align with **Hexagonal Architecture (aka Ports & Adapters)**.\n\n## 2. Decision\nEvery utility is implemented as a self-contained hexagon:\n\n1. **Domain/Core**  \n   * Pure POJOs and value objects  \n   * Use-case orchestrators (Application Services)  \n   * Port interfaces (inbound & outbound)\n\n2. **Adapters**  \n   * **Inbound**: REST controllers / GraphQL resolvers / message listeners  \n   * **Outbound**: Repository drivers, SaaS connectors, cache providers  \n\n3. **Configuration**  \n   * Wiring of ports to adapters via dependency injection (Spring Boot)  \n   * Uniform, versioned contracts exposed by the API Gateway layer  \n\nAll cross-cutting concerns (rate limiting, caching, tracing, etc.) are expressed as *decorators* or *framework interceptors*, never leaking into domain code.\n\n## 3. Consequences\n✔ Fast unit tests against core logic  \n✔ Easy to swap technologies (e.g., Caffeine → Redis)  \n✔ Clear test boundaries (mock ports instead of mocks everywhere)  \n✘ Slightly higher initial boilerplate (worth the ROI)\n\n---\n\n## 4. Reference Implementation\n\nBelow is a trimmed, yet **runnable** example of the File-Conversion utility adopting the hexagonal style.\n\n```java\n// -------- 1️⃣ Domain Layer (Core) -------------------------------------------\npackage com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain;\n\nimport java.time.OffsetDateTime;\nimport java.util.UUID;\n\n/**\n * Value object that represents a single file-conversion request.\n */\npublic record ConversionRequest(\n        UUID requestId,\n        String sourceFormat,\n        String targetFormat,\n        byte[] payload,\n        OffsetDateTime requestedAt\n) {}\n\n/**\n * Port that expresses the *purpose* of persisting conversion artefacts.\n */\npublic interface ArtefactStoragePort {\n    void save(UUID requestId, byte[] convertedPayload);\n}\n\n/**\n * Primary use-case orchestrator (Application Service).\n */\npublic class ConvertFileUseCase {\n\n    private final ArtefactStoragePort storagePort;\n    private final FileFormatConverter converter; // pure domain service\n\n    public ConvertFileUseCase(ArtefactStoragePort storagePort,\n                              FileFormatConverter converter) {\n        this.storagePort = storagePort;\n        this.converter   = converter;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Executes the conversion and persists the output using the outbound port.\n     */\n    public ConversionResponse execute(ConversionRequest request) {\n        byte[] output = converter.convert(\n                request.sourceFormat(),\n                request.targetFormat(),\n                request.payload()\n        );\n        storagePort.save(request.requestId(), output);\n        return new ConversionResponse(request.requestId(), output.length);\n    }\n}\n\n/**\n * Domain service that performs format conversion.\n * Decoupled from any IO concerns.\n */\npublic interface FileFormatConverter {\n    byte[] convert(String from, String to, byte[] input);\n}\n\n/**\n * Minimal response DTO emitted by the use-case.\n */\npublic record ConversionResponse(UUID requestId, int sizeInBytes) {}\n```\n\n```java\n// -------- 2️⃣ Inbound Adapter (REST Controller) -----------------------------\npackage com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.inbound.rest;\n\nimport com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain.*;\nimport jakarta.validation.constraints.NotNull;\nimport org.springframework.http.ResponseEntity;\nimport org.springframework.validation.annotation.Validated;\nimport org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.*;\n\nimport java.time.OffsetDateTime;\nimport java.util.UUID;\n\n/**\n * REST façade that delegates to the core use-case.\n */\n@RestController\n@RequestMapping(\"/v1/conversions\")\n@Validated\npublic class FileConversionController {\n\n    private final ConvertFileUseCase useCase;\n\n    public FileConversionController(ConvertFileUseCase useCase) {\n        this.useCase = useCase;\n    }\n\n    @PostMapping\n    public ResponseEntity<ConversionResponse> convert(\n            @RequestParam @NotNull String sourceFormat,\n            @RequestParam @NotNull String targetFormat,\n            @RequestBody byte[] payload\n    ) {\n        ConversionRequest request = new ConversionRequest(\n                UUID.randomUUID(),\n                sourceFormat,\n                targetFormat,\n                payload,\n                OffsetDateTime.now()\n        );\n        return ResponseEntity.ok(useCase.execute(request));\n    }\n}\n```\n\n```java\n// -------- 3️⃣ Outbound Adapter (S3 Artefact Storage) ------------------------\npackage com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.outbound.s3;\n\nimport com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain.ArtefactStoragePort;\nimport org.slf4j.Logger;\nimport org.slf4j.LoggerFactory;\nimport software.amazon.awssdk.core.sync.RequestBody;\nimport software.amazon.awssdk.services.s3.S3Client;\nimport software.amazon.awssdk.services.s3.model.PutObjectRequest;\n\nimport java.util.UUID;\n\n/**\n * Concrete adapter that stores conversion outputs in an S3 bucket.\n */\npublic class S3ArtefactStorageAdapter implements ArtefactStoragePort {\n\n    private static final Logger LOG = LoggerFactory.getLogger(S3ArtefactStorageAdapter.class);\n\n    private final S3Client s3;\n    private final String   bucketName;\n\n    public S3ArtefactStorageAdapter(S3Client s3, String bucketName) {\n        this.s3 = s3;\n        this.bucketName = bucketName;\n    }\n\n    @Override\n    public void save(UUID requestId, byte[] convertedPayload) {\n        try {\n            PutObjectRequest req = PutObjectRequest.builder()\n                    .bucket(bucketName)\n                    .key(requestId + \".bin\")\n                    .build();\n            s3.putObject(req, RequestBody.fromBytes(convertedPayload));\n        } catch (Exception e) {\n            LOG.error(\"Failed to store artefact for request {}\", requestId, e);\n            throw new ArtefactStorageException(requestId, e);\n        }\n    }\n}\n```\n\n```java\n// -------- 4️⃣ Configuration (Spring) ---------------------------------------\npackage com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.config;\n\nimport com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain.*;\nimport com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.outbound.s3.S3ArtefactStorageAdapter;\nimport org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.condition.ConditionalOnProperty;\nimport org.springframework.context.annotation.Bean;\nimport org.springframework.context.annotation.Configuration;\nimport software.amazon.awssdk.services.s3.S3Client;\n\n/**\n * Wires ports to adapters using Spring’s DI container.\n */\n@Configuration\npublic class FileConversionModule {\n\n    @Bean\n    ConvertFileUseCase convertFileUseCase(\n            ArtefactStoragePort storagePort,\n            FileFormatConverter converter) {\n        return new ConvertFileUseCase(storagePort, converter);\n    }\n\n    @Bean\n    @ConditionalOnProperty(name = \"storage.provider\", havingValue = \"s3\")\n    ArtefactStoragePort s3StoragePort(S3Client s3,\n                                      FileConversionProperties props) {\n        return new S3ArtefactStorageAdapter(s3, props.bucket());\n    }\n}\n```\n\n```java\n// -------- 5️⃣ Cross-Cutting Concern (Caching Decorator) --------------------\npackage com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.outbound.cache;\n\nimport com.github.benmanes.caffeine.cache.Cache;\nimport com.opsforge.utility.fileconversion.domain.ArtefactStoragePort;\n\nimport java.util.UUID;\n\n/**\n * Transparently caches artefacts before delegating to the \"real\" port.\n */\npublic class CachingStorageDecorator implements ArtefactStoragePort {\n\n    private final Cache<UUID, byte[]> cache;\n    private final ArtefactStoragePort delegate;\n\n    public CachingStorageDecorator(Cache<UUID, byte[]> cache,\n                                   ArtefactStoragePort delegate) {\n        this.cache = cache;\n        this.delegate = delegate;\n    }\n\n    @Override\n    public void save(UUID requestId, byte[] convertedPayload) {\n        cache.put(requestId, convertedPayload);\n        delegate.save(requestId, convertedPayload);\n    }\n}\n```\n\n## 5. Testing Strategy\n* **Core tests**: Pure JUnit tests verifying `ConvertFileUseCase` with mocked ports  \n* **Adapter tests**: Web-layer tests for controller, TestContainers for S3 emulation  \n* **Contract tests**: Swagger / GraphQL SDL snapshots per version\n\n---\n\n## 6. Rejected Alternatives\n1. Layered (n-tier) architecture — tight coupling across layers, harder to swap tech.  \n2. Anemic-model approach — domain logic scattered in services, violates DDD principles.  \n\n---\n\n## 7. Links\n* Alistair Cockburn, *Hexagonal Architecture* (2005)  \n* Fowler, *Ports and Adapters* article  \n```\n","numLines":287,"startLine":1,"totalLines":287}}}

infra-centric | Lacks domain validation; brittle error handling; Lua skillset scarcity |\n    34→| B | “Thin” GraphQL BFF per client | Flexible composition; schema-driven | Code duplication across BFFs; N+1 requests; operational overhead |\n    35→| C | Unified **Java** façade running in the same tech stack as utilities | Reuse common libs; type safety; Spring/WebFlux maturity | Increases JVM footprint; may appear monolithic |\n    36→\n    37→Empirical load-tests and maintainability reviews highlighted option **C** as the most sustainable: it maximises code reuse (error model, pagination, DTO mappers) and minimises operational sprawl.\n    38→\n    39→---\n    40→\n    41→## Decision\n    42→\n    43→We will implement a **unified Java-based façade service** that:\n    44→\n    45→1. Uses **Spring Boot 3.2** with **WebFlux** for non-blocking HTTP and **Netflix DGS** for GraphQL.\n    46→2. Implements **API-First** workflow via **OpenAPI 3.1** and **GraphQL SDL** living in `contracts/`.\n    47→3. Delegates all business logic to downstream utilities through **dedicated outbound ports** (Feign Client interfaces for REST, gRPC stubs, or JMS templates).\n    48→4. Applies **API versioning** through\n    49→   * URI segment (`/v{n}/…`) for REST  \n    50→   * Namespaced GraphQL schema (`schema @tag(name:\"v{n}\")`) for GraphQL\n    51→5. Encapsulates **cross-cutting filters** (auth, rate-limit, metrics, cache) using Spring `WebFilter` / `HandlerInterceptor` and DGS `DataFetcherInterceptor`.\n    52→6. Emits **structured problem details** (`application/problem+json`) conforming to [RFC 9457] for error handling.\n    53→7. Offers **partial responses & pagination** (RFC 7240 / Cursor-based) out of the box.\n    54→\n    55→---\n    56→\n    57→## Consequences\n    58→\n    59→### Positive\n    60→* **Consistency** – All clients (CLI, SDKs, UI) leverage the same semantics, reducing cognitive load.\n    61→* **Evolutionary** – Internals can migrate (REST → gRPC; Caffeine → Redis) without public contract churn.\n    62→* **Observability** – Central façade surfaces unified logs, tracing, and metrics, expediting SRE workflows.\n    63→* **Security** – Single choke-point to enforce JWT scopes, multi-tenancy guards, and threat mitigations.\n    64→\n    65→### Negative / Mitigations\n    66→* **Increased Blast Radius** – A defect in the façade could affect all utilities.  \n    67→  → Mitigation: Blue/Green deploys, exhaustive test pyramid, layered health-checks.\n    68→* **Throughput Bottleneck** – Additional hop may introduce latency.  \n    69→  → Mitigation: WebFlux back-pressure, response caching, and gRPC multiplexing for chatty paths.\n    70→* **Perceived Monolith** – May obscure microservice boundaries.  \n    71→  → Mitigation: Maintain clear ownership docs, contract tests, and align domain events per utility.\n    72→\n    73→---\n    74→\n    75→## Alternatives Considered\n    76→\n    77→* **GraphQL Federation** – Rejected due to premature complexity and lack of per-utility SDL maturity.\n    78→* **Service Mesh Sidecars** (Istio, Linkerd) – Suitable for transport-level concerns but insufficient for domain validation and versioning semantics.\n    79→* **Serverless Edge Functions** – Would optimise global latency but complicate stateful retries and bulk payload handling (>10 MB).\n    80→\n    81→---\n    82→\n    83→## Compliance & Reference Implementation\n    84→\n    85→All new endpoints **MUST**:\n    86→\n    87→1. Conform to the published OpenAPI/GraphQL contract before merge.  \n    88→2. Return a `problem+json` body for 4xx/5xx categories.  \n    89→3. Include `X-Request-Id`, `X-Correlation-Id` in all egress calls.  \n    90→4. Register with the façade’s central `UtilityRegistry` during application bootstrap.\n    91→\n    92→Reference code lives in `opsforge-facade/src/main/java/.../facade`.  \n    93→Sample usage:\n    94→\n    95→```java\n    96→@GetMapping(path = \"/v1/checksums/sha256\", produces = APPLICATION_JSON_VALUE)\n    97→public Mono<ChecksumResponse> calculateSha256(@Valid ChecksumRequest req) {\n    98→    return checksumPort.calculate(req)\n    99→                       .map(mapper::toResponse)\n   100→                       .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))\n   101→                       .onErrorMap(TimeoutException.class, ProblemDetails::fromTimeout);\n   102→}\n   103→```\n   104→\n   105→---\n   106→\n   107→## Next Steps\n   108→\n   109→1. Finalise façade v1 contract freeze (2024-05-15).\n   110→2. Migrate existing CLI to new endpoints.\n   111→3. Deprecate NGINX Lua scripts by Q3.\n   112→\n   113→---\n   114→\n   115→### Changelog\n   116→* 2024-04-27 – Initial decision recorded.\n   117→```\n   118→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. infra-centric | Lacks domain validation; brittle error handling; Lua skillset scarcity |\n| B | “Thin” GraphQL BFF per client | Flexible composition; schema-driven | Code duplication across BFFs; N+1 requests; operational overhead |\n| C | Unified **Java** façade running in the same tech stack as utilities | Reuse common libs; type safety; Spring/WebFlux maturity | Increases JVM footprint; may appear monolithic |\n\nEmpirical load-tests and maintainability reviews highlighted option **C** as the most sustainable: it maximises code reuse (error model, pagination, DTO mappers) and minimises operational sprawl.\n\n---\n\n## Decision\n\nWe will implement a **unified Java-based façade service** that:\n\n1. Uses **Spring Boot 3.2** with **WebFlux** for non-blocking HTTP and **Netflix DGS** for GraphQL.\n2. Implements **API-First** workflow via **OpenAPI 3.1** and **GraphQL SDL** living in `contracts/`.\n3. Delegates all business logic to downstream utilities through **dedicated outbound ports** (Feign Client interfaces for REST, gRPC stubs, or JMS templates).\n4. Applies **API versioning** through\n   * URI segment (`/v{n}/…`) for REST  \n   * Namespaced GraphQL schema (`schema @tag(name:\"v{n}\")`) for GraphQL\n5. Encapsulates **cross-cutting filters** (auth, rate-limit, metrics, cache) using Spring `WebFilter` / `HandlerInterceptor` and DGS `DataFetcherInterceptor`.\n6. Emits **structured problem details** (`application/problem+json`) conforming to [RFC 9457] for error handling.\n7. Offers **partial responses & pagination** (RFC 7240 / Cursor-based) out of the box.\n\n---\n\n## Consequences\n\n### Positive\n* **Consistency** – All clients (CLI, SDKs, UI) leverage the same semantics, reducing cognitive load.\n* **Evolutionary** – Internals can migrate (REST → gRPC; Caffeine → Redis) without public contract churn.\n* **Observability** – Central façade surfaces unified logs, tracing, and metrics, expediting SRE workflows.\n* **Security** – Single choke-point to enforce JWT scopes, multi-tenancy guards, and threat mitigations.\n\n### Negative / Mitigations\n* **Increased Blast Radius** – A defect in the façade could affect all utilities.  \n  → Mitigation: Blue/Green deploys, exhaustive test pyramid, layered health-checks.\n* **Throughput Bottleneck** – Additional hop may introduce latency.  \n  → Mitigation: WebFlux back-pressure, response caching, and gRPC multiplexing for chatty paths.\n* **Perceived Monolith** – May obscure microservice boundaries.  \n  → Mitigation: Maintain clear ownership docs, contract tests, and align domain events per utility.\n\n---\n\n## Alternatives Considered\n\n* **GraphQL Federation** – Rejected due to premature complexity and lack of per-utility SDL maturity.\n* **Service Mesh Sidecars** (Istio, Linkerd) – Suitable for transport-level concerns but insufficient for domain validation and versioning semantics.\n* **Serverless Edge Functions** – Would optimise global latency but complicate stateful retries and bulk payload handling (>10 MB).\n\n---\n\n## Compliance & Reference Implementation\n\nAll new endpoints **MUST**:\n\n1. Conform to the published OpenAPI/GraphQL contract before merge.  \n2. Return a `problem+json` body for 4xx/5xx categories.  \n3. Include `X-Request-Id`, `X-Correlation-Id` in all egress calls.  \n4. Register with the façade’s central `UtilityRegistry` during application bootstrap.\n\nReference code lives in `opsforge-facade/src/main/java/.../facade`.  \nSample usage:\n\n```java\n@GetMapping(path = \"/v1/checksums/sha256\", produces = APPLICATION_JSON_VALUE)\npublic Mono<ChecksumResponse> calculateSha256(@Valid ChecksumRequest req) {\n    return checksumPort.calculate(req)\n                       .map(mapper::toResponse)\n                       .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))\n                       .onErrorMap(TimeoutException.class, ProblemDetails::fromTimeout);\n}\n```\n\n---\n\n## Next Steps\n\n1. Finalise façade v1 contract freeze (2024-05-15).\n2. Migrate existing CLI to new endpoints.\n3. Deprecate NGINX Lua scripts by Q3.\n\n---\n\n### Changelog\n* 2024-04-27 – Initial decision recorded.\n```\n","numLines":118,"startLine":1,"totalLines":118}}}

/* ===== Invoke Use-Case ======================================================= */\n            ConversionJobResult conversionResult = fileConversionUseCase.convertFile(command);\n\n            /* ============================= */\n            processedMessageCache.put(jobId, Boolean.TRUE);\n            ack.acknowledge();\n\n            log.info(\"File-conversion job {} processed successfully\", jobId);\n\n        } catch (InvalidFormatException | JsonProcessingException e) {\n            log.error(\"Unable to deserialize message with key={}\", jobId, e);\n            publishFailure(jobId, error during processing of jobId {}\", jobId, e);\n            publishFailure(jobId, ERROR_CODE_PROCESSING, e.getMessage());\n            ack.acknowledge(); // Depending on retry policy we might nack instead\n        }\n    }\n\n    /* =====================================================================================\n       Private helper methods\n failure result for jobId {}\", jobId, e);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /* =====================================================================================\n       DTOs – These classes live in the static SimpleProfileBuilder builder() { return new SimpleProfileBuilder(); }\n\n        class SimpleProfileBuilder {\n            private final Map<String, Object> map = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n\n            public SimpleProfileBuilder put(String key, Object value) {\n                map.put(key, value);\n                return this;\n            }\n\n            public AnonymizationProfile build() {\n                return () -> Collections.unmodifiableMap(map);\n            }\n        }\n\n        Map<String, Object> parameters();\n\n        default String param(String key, String fallback) {\n            return Objects.toString(parameters().getOrDefault(key, fallback), fallback);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Column-level mapping for CSV anonymization.\n     */\n    @Getter\n    @RequiredArgsConstructor(access = AccessLevel.PRIVATE)\n    public static class ColumnRule {\n        private final String column;\n        private final DataType type;\n        private final AnonymizationProfile profile;\n\n        public static ColumnRule of(String column, DataType type, AnonymizationProfile profile) {\n            return new ColumnRule(column, type, profile);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Profile for CSV anonymization use case.\n     */\n    @Getter\n    @RequiredArgsConstructor\n    public static class CsvAnonymizationProfile {\n        private final List<ColumnRule> columnRules;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Dedicated unchecked exception for the anonymizer module.\n     */\n    public static class DataAnonymizationException extends RuntimeException {\n        public DataAnonymizationException(String message, Throwable cause) { super(message, cause); }\n    }\n\n    /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- */\n    /* ––––––––––––––––––– Sample internal anonymizer set ––––––––––––––––––– */\n    /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- */\n\n    /**\n     * Factory method that creates a default registry of anonymizers.\n     * Can be used by legacy code not relying on DI container.\n     */\n    public static DataAnonymizationService withDefaults() {\n        Map<DataType, GenericAnonymizer> registry = new EnumMap<>(DataType.class);\n\n        registry.put(DataType.EMAIL, new EmailAnonymizer());\n        registry.put(DataType.PHONE, new PhoneAnonymizer());\n        registry.put(DataType.FULL_NAME, new NameAnonymizer());\n        registry.put(DataType.GENERIC_STRING, (value, profile, loader) -> DigestUtils.sha256Hex(value));\n\n        return new DataAnonymizationService(registry);\n    }\n\n    /* ======================= Concrete strategies ======================== */\n\n    private static final class EmailAnonymizer implements GenericAnonymizer {\n\n        @Override\n        public String anonymize(String value,\n                                AnonymizationProfile profile,\n                                java.util.function.Function<String, Pattern> patternLoader) {\n\n            int atIdx = value.indexOf('@');\n            if (atIdx < 1) {\n                // Invalid email – hash instead\n                return DigestUtils.sha256Hex(value);\n            }\n            String domain = value.substring(atIdx);\n            String hash = DigestUtils.sha256Hex(value.substring(0, atIdx)).substring(0, 8);\n            return hash + domain;\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static final class PhoneAnonymizer implements GenericAnonymizer {\n\n        @Override\n        public String anonymize(String value,\n                                AnonymizationProfile profile,\n                                java.util.function.Function<String, Pattern> patternLoader) {\n\n            String digits = value.replaceAll(\"\\\\D\", \"\");\n            if (digits.length() < 4) {\n                return \"****\";\n            }\n            String last4 = digits.substring(digits.length() - 4);\n            return \"********\" + last4;\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static final class NameAnonymizer implements GenericAnonymizer {\n\n        private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n        private static final List<String> DICTIONARY = List.of(\n                \"Alpha\", \"Bravo\", \"Charlie\", \"Delta\", \"Echo\", \"Foxtrot\",\n                \"Golf\", \"Hotel\", \"India\", \"Juliet\", \"Kilo\", \"Lima\", \"Mike\",\n                \"November\", \"Oscar\", \"Papa\", \"Quebec\", \"Romeo\", \"Sierra\",\n                \"Tango\", \"Uniform\", \"Victor\", \"Whiskey\", \"Xray\", \"Yankee\", \"Zulu\");\n\n        @Override\n        public String anonymize(String value,\n                                AnonymizationProfile profile,\n                                java.util.function.Function<String, Pattern> patternLoader) {\n\n            // Preserve initials if requested, otherwise pick random callsign\n            boolean keepInitial = Boolean.parseBoolean(profile.param(\"keepInitial\", \"false\"));\n            String callsign = DICTIONARY.get(RANDOM.nextInt(DICTIONARY.size()));\n\n            return keepInitial && !value.isBlank()\n                    ? value.charAt(0) + \". \" + callsign\n                    : callsign;\n        }\n    }\n}","numLines":357,"startLine":1,"totalLines":357}}}

The feature enables users to submit complex, multi-step, asynchronous workflows that orchestrate operations across multiple utility services.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Current Architecture Summary\n\n### 1.1 Key Components and Their Roles\n\nBased on analysis of the codebase, the OpsForge Utility Nexus system consists of the following key architectural components:\n\n#### Infrastructure Components\n\n| Component | Location | Role |\n|-----------|----------|------|\n| **API Gateway** | `/app/project/api-gateway/` | Single entry-point exposing REST & GraphQL facades; handles cross-cutting concerns (rate-limiting, circuit-breaking, correlation-id propagation, routing) |\n| **Service Discovery** | `/app/project/service-discovery/` | Eureka-based service registry enabling dynamic service location (see `ServiceDiscoveryApplication.java:70-320`) |\n| **Config Server** | `/app/project/config-server/` | Spring Cloud Config Server providing centralized, versioned configuration to all microservices (see `ConfigServerApplication.java:48-225`) |\n| **Common Library** | `/app/project/common-library/` | Shared kernel containing cross-cutting concerns: logging, error responses, security utilities |\n\n#### Utility Microservices\n\n| Service | Location | Purpose |\n|---------|----------|---------|\n| **File Converter Service** | `/app/project/utility-services/file-converter-service/` | Format conversion (PDF, DOCX, CSV, JSON, etc.) |\n| **Data Anonymizer Service** | `/app/project/utility-services/data-anonymizer-service/` | PII anonymization for JSON and CSV data |\n| **Checksum Generator Service** | `/app/project/utility-services/checksum-generator-service/` | Cryptographic hash generation |\n| **Scheduler Service** | `/app/project/utility-services/scheduler-service/` | Time-zone aware scheduling |\n| **Text Transformer Service** | `/app/project/utility-services/text-transformer-service/` | Bulk text transformations |\n\n### 1.2 Architectural Patterns in Use\n\n#### Microservices Architecture (ADR-001)\nAs documented in `/app/project/docs/adr/001-architectural-style-microservices.md`:\n- Each utility is an independent, deployable microservice\n- Services own their hexagonal architecture internals\n- Communication through well-defined, versioned REST contracts\n- Domain events published for eventual consistency\n\n#### Hexagonal Architecture (ADR-002)\nAs documented in `/app/project/docs/adr/002-hexagonal-architecture-for-services.md`, each service follows the Ports & Adapters pattern:\n\n```\nService Structure (e.g., file-converter-service):\n├── adapter/\n│   ├── in/\n│   │   ├── web/           # Inbound REST adapter (FileConversionController.java)\n│   │   └── messaging/     # Inbound Kafka adapter (FileConversionMessageListener.java)\n│   └── out/\n│       ├── persistence/   # Outbound JPA adapter\n│       ├── cache/         # Outbound Caffeine adapter\n│       └── monitoring/    # Outbound metrics adapter\n├── domain/\n│   ├── model/             # Domain entities (ConversionRequest, ConversionResult)\n│   ├── port/\n│   │   ├── in/            # Inbound ports (FileConversionUseCase.java)\n│   │   └── out/           # Outbound ports (ConversionAuditPort, MetricsEmitterPort)\n│   └── service/           # Domain services (FileConversionService.java)\n└── config/                # Spring configuration (BeanConfiguration.java)\n```\n\n#### API Facade Strategy (ADR-003)\nAs documented in `/app/project/docs/adr/003-api-facade-strategy.md`:\n- Unified Java-based facade using Spring Boot 3.2 + WebFlux\n- API versioning through URI segments (`/v{n}/...`)\n- Cross-cutting filters for auth, rate-limiting, metrics, caching\n\n### 1.3 Current Communication Patterns\n\nThe system currently supports two communication patterns:\n\n#### 1.3.1 Synchronous REST Communication\n- Primary pattern for client-facing interactions\n- API Gateway routes requests to downstream services via `GatewayRouteConfig.java:58-131`\n- Each service exposes REST endpoints (e.g., `FileConversionController.java:44-174`)\n- Circuit breakers and rate limiting applied at gateway level\n\n**Example Flow (File Conversion)**:\n```\nClient → API Gateway (/api/v1/convert/**)\n       → FILE-CONVERSION-SERVICE (lb://FILE-CONVERSION-SERVICE)\n       → FileConversionController.convertFile()\n       → FileConversionUseCase.convert()\n       → Response\n```\n\n#### 1.3.2 Asynchronous Messaging (Kafka)\n- Used for long-running operations and event-driven workflows\n- `FileConversionMessageListener.java:51-249` demonstrates:\n  - Kafka listener for async conversion requests\n  - Idempotency via Caffeine-based deduplication cache\n  - Result publication to dedicated reply topic\n  - Manual acknowledgment for at-least-once delivery\n\n**Evidence from codebase**:\n```java\n// FileConversionMessageListener.java:92-99\n@KafkaListener(\n    topics = \"${nexus.messaging.topics.file-conversion-requests}\",\n    groupId = \"${spring.kafka.consumer.group-id}\",\n    containerFactory = \"kafkaListenerContainerFactory\"\n)\npublic void onMessage(final ConsumerRecord<String, String> record,\n                      final Acknowledgment ack,\n                      final Consumer<?, ?> consumer) { ... }\n```\n\n---\n\n## 2. Proposal: Pipeline Orchestrator Service\n\n### 2.1 Architectural Trade-off Analysis: Orchestration vs. Choreography\n\nFor implementing multi-step workflows, two primary patterns exist:\n\n#### Choreography Pattern\n\n**How it works**: Services react to events autonomously. Each service publishes an event upon completion, and the next service in the chain subscribes to that event.\n\n**Example Flow (Anonymize → Convert)**:\n```\nClient → Anonymizer Service → publishes \"AnonymizationComplete\" event\n                            → File Converter subscribes → processes → publishes \"ConversionComplete\"\n                            → Client polls or receives callback\n```\n\n**Pros**:\n- Loose coupling between services\n- Services remain autonomous\n- Easy to add new event consumers\n\n**Cons**:\n- **Implicit workflow**: The overall process is distributed across services, making it hard to understand and debug\n- **State management**: No central place to track job progress; each service only knows its part\n- **Error handling complexity**: If step 2 of 3 fails, implementing compensation/rollback is challenging\n- **Monitoring difficulty**: Requires correlation IDs and distributed tracing to follow a job's path\n\n#### Orchestration Pattern (Recommended)\n\n**How it works**: A central orchestrator explicitly manages the workflow, calling each service in sequence and maintaining job state.\n\n**Example Flow (Anonymize → Convert)**:\n```\nClient → Pipeline Orchestrator Service\n       → (1) Calls Anonymizer Service → stores intermediate result\n       → (2) Calls File Converter Service → stores final result\n       → Updates job state at each step\n       → Client polls for status/result\n```\n\n**Pros**:\n- **Explicit workflow**: The entire process is visible in one place\n- **Centralized state**: Job progress, status, and results are managed uniformly\n- **Error handling**: Clear location for retries, compensation, and failure logging\n- **Monitoring**: Single service exposes job status, making dashboards straightforward\n- **Extensibility**: Easy to add new steps, conditional logic, or parallel execution\n\n**Cons**:\n- Orchestrator is tightly coupled to workflow logic\n- Single point of failure (mitigated by standard resilience patterns)\n\n### 2.2 Recommendation: Orchestration\n\nFor a **defined, stateful, multi-step business process** like the Data Processing Pipeline, **orchestration is the superior pattern**. The explicit nature of the workflow, combined with the need for robust state management and error handling, makes a central orchestrator the right choice.\n\n### 2.3 Proposed Solution: New `pipeline-orchestrator-service`\n\n#### Why a New Service?\n\n1. **Separation of Concerns**: Pipeline orchestration is a distinct capability from the individual utilities\n2. **Bounded Context**: The pipeline domain (jobs, steps, state transitions) is separate from conversion/anonymization domains\n3. **Scalability**: The orchestrator can scale independently based on pipeline job volume\n4. **Consistency**: Follows the existing pattern where each capability is a separate microservice\n\n#### Service Architecture\n\nThe new service will follow the established Hexagonal Architecture:\n\n```\npipeline-orchestrator-service/\n├── src/main/java/com/opsforge/nexus/pipeline/\n│   ├── adapter/\n│   │   ├── in/\n│   │   │   └── web/\n│   │   │       ├── PipelineController.java          # REST endpoints\n│   │   │       └── dto/\n│   │   │           ├── CreatePipelineJobRequest.java\n│   │   │           ├── PipelineJobStatusResponse.java\n│   │   │           └── PipelineStepDto.java\n│   │   └── out/\n│   │       ├── persistence/\n│   │       │   ├── JpaPipelineJobRepository.java    # PostgreSQL adapter\n│   │       │   └── entity/\n│   │       │       ├── PipelineJobEntity.java\n│   │       │       └── PipelineStepEntity.java\n│   │       └── service/\n│   │           ├── AnonymizerServiceClient.java     # HTTP client adapter\n│   │           ├── FileConverterServiceClient.java  # HTTP client adapter\n│   │           └── ChecksumServiceClient.java       # HTTP client adapter\n│   ├── domain/\n│   │   ├── model/\n│   │   │   ├── PipelineJob.java                     # Aggregate root\n│   │   │   ├── PipelineStep.java                    # Value object\n│   │   │   ├── JobStatus.java                       # Enum\n│   │   │   └── StepDefinition.java                  # Step configuration\n│   │   ├── port/\n│   │   │   ├── in/\n│   │   │   │   └── PipelineOrchestrationUseCase.java  # Inbound port\n│   │   │   └── out/\n│   │   │       ├── PipelineJobRepository.java        # Outbound port\n│   │   │       ├── AnonymizerPort.java               # Outbound port\n│   │   │       └── FileConverterPort.java            # Outbound port\n│   │   └── service/\n│   │       └── PipelineOrchestrationService.java    # Domain service\n│   └── config/\n│       ├── BeanConfiguration.java\n│       └── AsyncConfig.java\n├── src/main/resources/\n│   ├── application.yml\n│   └── bootstrap.yml\n└── pom.xml\n```\n\n---\n\n## 3. Detailed Data Flow: Anonymize-then-Convert Pipeline\n\n### 3.1 End-to-End Flow Diagram\n\n```\n┌─────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────┐\n│ Client  │────▶│ API Gateway │────▶│ Pipeline Orchestrator    │\n└─────────┘     └─────────────┘     │        Service           │\n     │                              └──────────────────────────┘\n     │                                          │\n     │                                          │ (1) Create Job\n     │                                          │     (async)\n     │                                          ▼\n     │                              ┌──────────────────────────┐\n     │                              │     PostgreSQL           │\n     │                              │   (Job State Store)      │\n     │                              └──────────────────────────┘\n     │                                          │\n     │          ┌───────────────────────────────┘\n     │          │\n     │          │ (2) Execute Step 1: Anonymize\n     │          ▼\n     │   ┌──────────────────────────┐\n     │   │ Data Anonymizer Service  │\n     │   └──────────────────────────┘\n     │          │\n     │          │ (3) Store intermediate result\n     │          │     Update job state\n     │          ▼\n     │   ┌──────────────────────────┐\n     │   │     PostgreSQL / S3      │\n     │   │  (Intermediate Storage)  │\n     │   └──────────────────────────┘\n     │          │\n     │          │ (4) Execute Step 2: Convert\n     │          ▼\n     │   ┌──────────────────────────┐\n     │   │ File Converter Service   │\n     │   └──────────────────────────┘\n     │          │\n     │          │ (5) Store final result\n     │          │     Update job state to COMPLETED\n     │          ▼\n     │   ┌──────────────────────────┐\n     │   │     PostgreSQL / S3      │\n     │   │    (Result Storage)      │\n     │   └──────────────────────────┘\n     │\n     │  (6) Poll for status / Download result\n     └─────────────────────────────────────────▶\n```\n\n### 3.2 Step-by-Step Description\n\n#### Step 1: Client Submits Pipeline Job\n\n**Request**:\n```http\nPOST /api/v1/pipelines/jobs\nHost: api-gateway:8080\nContent-Type: application/json\nAuthorization: Bearer <jwt-token>\nX-Correlation-Id: abc-123\n\n{\n  \"name\": \"customer-data-processing\",\n  \"steps\": [\n    {\n      \"type\": \"ANONYMIZE\",\n      \"config\": {\n        \"rules\": {\n          \"email\": \"MASK\",\n          \"phone\": \"HASH\",\n          \"ssn\": \"NULLIFY\"\n        }\n      }\n    },\n    {\n      \"type\": \"CONVERT\",\n      \"config\": {\n        \"targetFormat\": \"PARQUET\"\n      }\n    }\n  ],\n  \"inputData\": {\n    \"sourceType\": \"INLINE\",\n    \"format\": \"CSV\",\n    \"content\": \"base64-encoded-csv-data\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\n**Response** (Immediate):\n```http\nHTTP/1.1 202 Accepted\nLocation: /api/v1/pipelines/jobs/job-uuid-12345\nX-Correlation-Id: abc-123\n\n{\n  \"jobId\": \"job-uuid-12345\",\n  \"status\": \"PENDING\",\n  \"createdAt\": \"2024-01-15T10:30:00Z\",\n  \"steps\": [\n    { \"stepIndex\": 0, \"type\": \"ANONYMIZE\", \"status\": \"PENDING\" },\n    { \"stepIndex\": 1, \"type\": \"CONVERT\", \"status\": \"PENDING\" }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n#### Step 2: Orchestrator Executes Anonymization\n\nThe orchestrator (running asynchronously via `@Async` executor):\n\n1. **Updates job state** to `IN_PROGRESS`\n2. **Updates step 0 state** to `IN_PROGRESS`\n3. **Calls Anonymizer Service**:\n   ```http\n   POST /api/v1/anonymizer/json\n   Host: data-anonymizer-service:8091\n   X-Correlation-Id: abc-123\n\n   {\n     \"payload\": { ... csv as json ... },\n     \"rules\": { \"email\": \"MASK\", \"phone\": \"HASH\", \"ssn\": \"NULLIFY\" }\n   }\n   ```\n4. **Stores intermediate result** (anonymized data) in object storage or database\n5. **Updates step 0 state** to `COMPLETED`\n\n#### Step 3: Orchestrator Executes Conversion\n\n1. **Updates step 1 state** to `IN_PROGRESS`\n2. **Retrieves intermediate result** from storage\n3. **Calls File Converter Service**:\n   ```http\n   POST /api/v1/convert\n   Host: file-converter-service:8082\n   X-Correlation-Id: abc-123\n   Content-Type: multipart/form-data\n\n   file: <anonymized-data>\n   targetFormat: PARQUET\n   ```\n4. **Stores final result** (converted file)\n5. **Updates step 1 state** to `COMPLETED`\n6. **Updates job state** to `COMPLETED`\n\n#### Step 4: Client Polls for Status\n\n**Request**:\n```http\nGET /api/v1/pipelines/jobs/job-uuid-12345\nAuthorization: Bearer <jwt-token>\n```\n\n**Response** (Completed):\n```json\n{\n  \"jobId\": \"job-uuid-12345\",\n  \"status\": \"COMPLETED\",\n  \"createdAt\": \"2024-01-15T10:30:00Z\",\n  \"completedAt\": \"2024-01-15T10:31:45Z\",\n  \"steps\": [\n    { \"stepIndex\": 0, \"type\": \"ANONYMIZE\", \"status\": \"COMPLETED\", \"duration\": \"45s\" },\n    { \"stepIndex\": 1, \"type\": \"CONVERT\", \"status\": \"COMPLETED\", \"duration\": \"60s\" }\n  ],\n  \"result\": {\n    \"downloadUrl\": \"/api/v1/pipelines/jobs/job-uuid-12345/result\",\n    \"checksum\": \"sha256:abc123...\",\n    \"sizeBytes\": 524288\n  }\n}\n```\n\n#### Step 5: Client Downloads Result\n\n```http\nGET /api/v1/pipelines/jobs/job-uuid-12345/result\nAuthorization: Bearer <jwt-token>\n\nHTTP/1.1 200 OK\nContent-Type: application/octet-stream\nContent-Disposition: attachment; filename=\"customer-data-processed.parquet\"\n```\n\n### 3.3 Error Handling Flow\n\nIf step 2 (Conversion) fails:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"jobId\": \"job-uuid-12345\",\n  \"status\": \"FAILED\",\n  \"createdAt\": \"2024-01-15T10:30:00Z\",\n  \"failedAt\": \"2024-01-15T10:31:30Z\",\n  \"steps\": [\n    { \"stepIndex\": 0, \"type\": \"ANONYMIZE\", \"status\": \"COMPLETED\" },\n    {\n      \"stepIndex\": 1,\n      \"type\": \"CONVERT\",\n      \"status\": \"FAILED\",\n      \"error\": {\n        \"code\": \"UNSUPPORTED_CONVERSION\",\n        \"message\": \"Cannot convert JSON to PARQUET: schema inference failed\"\n      }\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n---\n\n## 4. Impact Analysis: New and Modified Files\n\n### 4.1 New Files to Create\n\n#### New Module: `pipeline-orchestrator-service`\n\n| File Path | Description |\n|-----------|-------------|\n| */\n.route(\"pipeline-orchestrator-v1\", r -> r\n        .path(\"/api/v1/pipelines/**\")\n        .filters(f -> f\n                .stripPrefix(2)\n                .filter(new CorrelationIdGatewayFilter())\n                .requestRateLimiter(c -> c\n                        .setRateLimiter(redisRateLimiter)\n                        .setKeyResolver(userKeyResolver)\n                        .setStatusCode(HttpStatus.TOO_MANY_REQUESTS))\n                .circuitBreaker(cb -> cb\n                        .setName(\"pipelineOrchestratorCircuitBreaker\")\n definition:\n\n```yaml\npipeline-orchestrator-service:\n  image: opsforge/pipeline-orchestrator-service:1.0.0\n  container_name: pipeline-orchestrator-service\n  build:\n    context: ./utility-services/pipeline-orchestrator-service\n    dockerfile: Dockerfile\n  env_file:\n    - ./env/pipeline-orchestrator.env\n  restart: unless-stopped\n  depends_on:\n    postgres:\n      condition: service_healthy\n    discovery:\n      condition: service_started\n    data-anonymization-service:\n      condition: service_healthy\n    file-conversion-service:\n      condition: service_healthy\n  ports:\n    - \"8095:8095\"\n  healthcheck:\n    test: [\"CMD\", \"curl\", \"-f\", \"http://localhost:8095/actuator/health\"]\n    interval: 30s\n    timeout: 10s\n    retries: 3\n  networks:\n    - backend\n```\n\n#### 4.3.3 utility-services/pom.xml (Modified)\n\nAdd module declaration:\n\n```xml\n<modules>\n    <module>file-converter-service</module>\n Alignment with Existing Architectural Patterns\n\nThe proposed solution aligns with all established patterns:\n\n| Pattern | How the Proposal Aligns |\n|---------|------------------------|\n| **Microservices (ADR-001)** | New independent service with own lifecycle, scalability, and deployment |\n| **Hexagonal Architecture (ADR-002)** | Clear separation of domain, inbound adapters (REST), and outbound adapters (HTTP clients, JPA) |\n| **API Facade Strategy (ADR-003)** | Routes through API Gateway with consistent versioning (`/api/v1/pipelines/**`) |\n| **Service Discovery** | Registers with Eureka (`@EnableDiscoveryClient`) |\n| **Config Server** | Pulls configuration from centralized Config Server via `bootstrap.yml` |\n| **Observability** | Integrates with existing Prometheus/Grafana/Zipkin stack |\n| **Resilience** | Uses Resilience4j for circuit breaking on outbound HTTP calls |\n\n---\n\n## 6. Key Design Decisions\n\n### 6.1 Asynchronous Job Processing\n\nJobs are processed asynchronously using Spring's `@Async` with a dedicated thread pool:\n\n```java\n@Configuration\n@EnableAsync\npublic class AsyncConfig {\n    @Bean(\"pipelineExecutor\")\n    public Executor pipelineExecutor() {\n        ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor = new ThreadPoolTaskExecutor();\n        executor.setCorePoolSize(10);\n        executor.setMaxPoolSize(50);\n        executor.setQueueCapacity(100);\n        executor.setThreadNamePrefix(\"pipeline-\");\n        executor.initialize();\n        return executor;\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### 6.2 State Persistence\n\nJob state is persisted to PostgreSQL to survive orchestrator restarts:\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE pipeline_jobs (\n    id UUID PRIMARY KEY,\n    name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,\n    status VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,\n    created_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,\n    updated_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,\n    completed_at TIMESTAMP,\n    correlation_id VARCHAR(100),\n    input_data_ref VARCHAR(500)\n);\n\nCREATE TABLE pipeline_steps (\n    id UUID PRIMARY KEY,\n    job_id UUID REFERENCES pipeline_jobs(id),\n    step_index INT NOT NULL,\n    step_type VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,\n    status VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,\n    config JSONB,\n    started_at TIMESTAMP,\n    completed_at TIMESTAMP,\n    output_ref VARCHAR(500),\n    error_message TEXT\n);\n```\n\n### 6.3 Intermediate Data Storage\n\nFor intermediate results between pipeline steps:\n- Small payloads (<1MB): Store in PostgreSQL JSONB column\n- Large payloads: Store in S3-compatible object storage (MinIO in development)\n\n### 6.4 Idempotency\n\nJob creation is idempotent using client-provided `idempotencyKey`:\n\n```java\n@PostMapping(\"/jobs\")\npublic ResponseEntity<PipelineJobStatusResponse> createJob(\n    @RequestHeader(value = \"Idempotency-Key\", required = false) String idempotencyKey,\n    @RequestBody CreatePipelineJobRequest request) {\n\n    if (idempotencyKey != null) {\n        Optional<PipelineJob> existing = jobRepository.findByIdempotencyKey(idempotencyKey);\n        if (existing.isPresent()) {\n            return ResponseEntity.ok(mapToResponse(existing.get()));\n        }\n    }\n    // ... create new job\n}\n```\n\n---\n\n## 7. Conclusion\n\nThis architectural proposal introduces a new `pipeline-orchestrator-service` that:\n\n1. **Follows existing patterns**: Hexagonal architecture, microservices, API facade strategy\n2. **Uses orchestration over choreography**: Provides explicit, manageable, and monitorable workflows\n3. **Minimizes impact**: Only requires adding a new service module and updating gateway routes\n4. **Enables extensibility**: Easy to add new step types, conditional logic, or parallel execution\n\nThe solution leverages the existing infrastructure (Service Discovery, Config Server, API Gateway) while introducing workflow orchestration as a first-class capability.\n\n---\n\n## References\n\n- ADR-001: `/app/project/docs/adr/001-architectural-style-microservices.md`\n- ADR-002: `/app/project/docs/adr/002-hexagonal-architecture-for-services.md`\n- ADR-003: `/app/project/docs/adr/003-api-facade-strategy.md`\n- File Converter Service: `/app/project/utility-services/file-converter-service/`\n- Data Anonymizer Service: `/app/project/utility-services/data-anonymizer-service/`\n- API Gateway Routes: `/app/project/api-gateway/src/main/java/com/opsforge/nexus/gateway/config/GatewayRouteConfig.java`\n- Docker Compose: `/app/project/docker-compose.yml`\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":0,"cache_creation_input_tokens":105,"cache_read_input_tokens":97000,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":105,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":25,"service_tier":"standard"},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"6e40b04a-4c07-4ffd-908b-aa0f7bc54884","uuid":"12492c94-d542-4ed4-91fa-d4c4b2010deb"}
